Uniform fluctuation and wandering bounds in first passage percolation
Kenneth S. Alexander

Abstract
. We consider first passage percolation on certain isotropic random graphs in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . We assume exponential concentration of passage times T ⁡ ( x , y ) T(x,y) , on some scale σ r \sigma_{r} whenever | y − x | |y-x| is of order r r , with σ r \sigma_{r} “growning like r χ r^{\chi} ” for some 0 < χ < 1 0<\chi<1 . Heuristically this means transverse wandering of geodesics should be at most of order Δ r = ( r ​ σ r ) 1 / 2 \Delta_{r}=(r\sigma_{r})^{1/2} . We show that in fact uniform versions of exponential concentration and wandering bounds hold: except with probability exponentially small in t t , there are no x , y x,y in a natural cylinder of length r r and radius K ​ Δ r K\Delta_{r} for which either (i) | T ⁡ ( x , y ) − E ​ T ​ ( x , y ) | ≥ t ​ σ r |T(x,y)-ET(x,y)|\geq t\sigma_{r} , or (ii) the geodesic from x x to y y wanders more than distance t ​ Δ r \sqrt{t}\Delta_{r} from the cylinder axis. We also establish that for the time constant μ = lim n E ​ T ​ ( 0 , n ​ e 1 ) / n \mu=\lim_{n}ET(0,ne_{1})/n , the “nonrandom error” | μ | ​ x ​ | − E ​ T ​ ( 0 , x ) | |\mu|x|-ET(0,x)| is at most a constant multiple of σ ⁡ ( | x | ) \sigma(|x|) .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
60K35 Primary 82B43 Secondary

1.
Introduction.

In i.i.d. first passage percolation (FPP) on a graph
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
, i.i.d.
(edge) passage times

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}t_{e}}
are attached to the edges
e\in\mathbb{E}
, and for a path
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{G}
, the
(path) passage time

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T(\Gamma)}
is the sum of the times
t_{e}
over
e\in\Gamma
. For
x,y\in\mathbb{V}
, the
passage time
from
x
to
y
is

(1.1)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T(x,y)}=\inf\{T(\Gamma):\Gamma\text{ is a path from $x$ to $y$ in $\mathbb{G}$}\}.

The
geodesic
from
x
to
y
is the path, denoted
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma_{xy}}
, which minimizes the path passage time; when
t_{e}
is a continuous random variable (as we always assume), a unique geodesic exists a.s.
[
17
]
.

There are two exponents of primary interest in the study of FPP. First, the fluctuations (i.e. standard deviation) of passage times
T(x,y)
for
|y-x|
of scale
r
in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
are believed to be of order
r^{\chi}
for some
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\chi}=\chi_{d}<1/2
, with
\chi_{2}=1/3
. Second, the typical transverse wandering of a geodesic, meaning the maximum distance from any point on
\Gamma_{xy}
to the straight line (denoted
\Pi_{xy}
) from
x
to
y
, is believed to be of order
r^{\xi}
for some
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\xi}=\xi_{d}
. For
|y-x|
of order
r
, if
\Gamma_{xy}
contains a vertex
z
at distance of order
r^{\xi}
from
\Pi_{xy}
(not too near
x
or
y
), then the associated extra distance
|z-x|+|y-z|-|y-x|
traveled by the geodesic in order to pass through
z
is of order
r^{2\xi-1}
. For such wandering to have non-negligible probability, the passage time fluctuations
r^{\chi}
should be at least as large as the extra distance; heuristically this leads to the relation
\chi=2\xi-1
. There are various ways to formally define the exponents
\chi,\xi
; these must allow for the fact that the true scales of fluctuations and wandering are not known to be pure powers of
r
. Chatterjee
[
9
]
gave a rigorous version of the relationship
\chi=2\xi-1
, under the assumption that multiple possible definitions of each exponent actually agree.

Looking more finely than just at the level of exponents, the heuristic for
\chi=2\xi-1
says that if the fluctuation scale is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma_{r}}
for
|y-x|
of order
r
, then the scale of transverse wandering should be

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Delta_{r}}=\Delta(r)=(r\sigma_{r})^{1/2}.

In
[
2
]
and (for
d=2
) in
[
14
]
it was shown that under natural assumptions, the transverse wandering with high probability does not exceed
(r\sigma_{r}\log r)^{1/2}
. One of our main results here is an upper bound on wandering: under somewhat weaker assumptions, for all
d\geq 2
, the probability of wandering greater than
s\Delta_{r}
decays as
e^{-cs^{2}}
or faster, for
s\leq r/\Delta_{r}
. Previously such a result has only been known for integrable cases of last passage percolation (LPP) in
d=2
, from
[
8
]
(with
e^{-cs}
in place of
e^{-cs^{2}}
) and
[
7
]
. The bound is optimal in the sense of being on the scale
\Delta_{r}
, though the second power of
s
in the exponent may not be optimal, as suggested by LPP results in
d=2
in
[
15
]
.

In fact we have this bound uniformly over many geodesics simultaneously, in the following sense: Consider a cylinder of length
r
and radius
K\Delta_{r}
, and let
\epsilon>0
and
s>2K
. Then under the assumptions we will make, the probability that there exists
any
geodesic
\Gamma_{xy}
, with
x,y
in the cylinder and
|y-x|\geq\epsilon r
, which wanders farther than
s\Delta_{r}
from the cylinder axis decays as
e^{-cs^{2}}
or faster, for
s\leq r/\Delta_{r}
. The scale
\Delta_{r}
here is optimal, though the decay
e^{-cs^{2}}
may not be, based on LPP results for
d=2
in
[
15
]
.

By comparison, in
[
2
]
it was shown roughly that if there is exponential concentration on some scale
\sigma_{r}
which “grows like a power of
r
,” uniformly for passage times over distance
r
, then the probability of a transverse fluctuation of size
t(\log r)^{1/2}\Delta_{r}
for a
single
geodesic
\Gamma_{xy}
is bounded by
C_{1}e^{-C_{2}t^{2}\log t}
for all
t>0
. This tells us nothing, though, about transverse fluctuations of size
t\Delta_{r}
with
1\ll t\ll(\log r)^{1/2}
, which should also be subject to exponential concentration, as in our present result.

It should be emphasized that in our transverse wandering (and other) results,
\sigma_{r}
is not necessarily the actual scale of the standard deviation—it need only be an upper bound in the sense that exponential concentration holds for passage times
T(x,y)
on scale
\sigma(|y-x|)
. Then the corresponding value
\Delta_{r}
is the scale that appears in the upper bound for transverse wandering.

Our uniform wandering bound will be a byproduct of another uniform–bound result for passage times; to describe it we first discuss exponential bounds.
For the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, Kesten
[
18
]
proved that, assuming

(1.2)

Ee^{\lambda t_{e}}<\infty\ \text{for some $\lambda>0$, \hskip 14.22636ptand }\quad P(t_{e}=0)<p_{c}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})

(where
p_{c}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
is the bond percolation threshold for
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
), there is exponential concentration of
T(x,y)
on scale
r^{1/2}
for
|x-y|\leq r
:

P(|T(x,y)-ET(x,y)|\geq tr^{1/2})\leq C_{3}e^{-C_{4}t}\quad\text{for all }t\leq C_{5}r.

Talagrand
[
26
]
improved this: assuming just an exponential moment for
t_{e}
,

P(|T(x,y)-ET(x,y)|\geq tr^{1/2})\leq C_{6}e^{-C_{7}\min(t^{2},tr^{1/2})}\quad\text{for all }t>0.

Damron, Hanson, and Sosoe
[
11
]
improved the bound to a subgaussian scale: under (
1.2
),

P\left(|T(x,y)-ET(x,y)|\geq t\left(\frac{r}{\log r}\right)^{1/2}\right)\leq C_{8}e^{-C_{9}t}\quad\text{for all }t>0.

None of these bounds are near–optimal, though—an optimal bound would be on the scale of the standard deviation of
T(x,y)
. What we will prove here is roughly as follows. Suppose passage times satisfy exponential concentration on a scale
\sigma(\cdot)
, uniformly:

(1.3)

P\Big(|T(x,y)-ET(x,y)|\geq t\sigma(|y-x|)\Big)\leq C_{10}e^{-C_{11}t}\quad\text{for all }x,y,

for some
\sigma(r)
which “grows like
r^{\chi}
” for some
\chi\in(0,1)
, in a sense we will make precise. Then for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}G_{r}(K)}
a cylinder of length
r
and radius
K\Delta_{r}
for some fixed
K
, we have concentration on the same scale, uniformly over
x,y\in G_{r}(K)
:

(1.4)

P\Big(\big|T(x,y)-ET(x,y)\big|\geq t\sigma_{r}\text{ for some $x,y\in G_{K}(r)$ with }|y-x|\geq\epsilon r\Big)\leq C_{12}e^{-C_{13}t}

for all
r
large and
t\geq cK^{2}
. This has previously been proved for integrable models of LPP in
d=2
(
[
8
]
,
[
6
]
), but even the non-integrable part of the proof there does not carry over to FPP—see Remark
1.8
.

For
d\geq 3
there is no generally-agreed-upon value of
\chi
in the physics literature. Heuristics and simulations suggest that
\chi
should decrease with dimension; simulations in
[
25
]
for a model believed to be in the same (KPZ) universality class as FPP show a decrease from
\chi=.33
to
\chi=.054
as
d
increases from 2 to 7. Some have predicted the existence of a finite upper critical dimension, possibly as low as 3.5, above which
\chi=0
(
[
13
]
,
[
20
]
); others predict that
\chi
is positive for all
d
(
[
3
]
,
[
24
]
), with simulations in
[
19
]
showing
\chi>0
all the way to
d=12
, decaying approximately as
1/(d+1)
. Our results here require
\chi>0
so they only have content below the upper critical dimension, should it be finite.

In the preceding and throughout the paper,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c_{1},c_{2},\dots}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}C_{1},C_{2},\dots}
, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\dots}
represent unspecified constants which depend only on the graph
\mathbb{G}
(or its distribution, if it is random) and the distribution of the passage times
t_{e}
(or speeds
\eta_{e}
, to be given below.) We use
C_{i}
for constants which occur outside of proofs and may be referenced later; any given
C_{i}
has the same value at all occurrences. We use
c_{i}
for those which do not recur and are only needed inside one proof. For the
c_{i}
’s we restart the numbering with
c_{0}
in each proof, and the values are different in different proofs.

As is standard, since passage times
T(x,y)
are subadditive, assumptions much weaker than (
1.2
) guarantee the a.s. existence (positive and finite for
x\neq 0
) of the limit

(1.5)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}g(x)}=\lim_{n}\frac{T(0,nx)}{n}=\lim_{n}\frac{ET(0,nx)}{n}=\inf_{n}\frac{ET(0,nx)}{n}\quad\text{a.s.~and in }L^{1}

for
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
;
g
extends to
x
with rational coordinates by considering only
n
with
nx\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and then to a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by uniform continuity. We write
\mathfrak{B}_{g}
for the unit ball of this norm. To obtain the optimal uniform results for wandering and (
1.4
) for fluctuations, we need to understand both parts of the discrepancy

(1.6)

T(0,x)-g(x)=\Big(T(0,x)-ET(0,x)\Big)+\Big(ET(0,x)-g(x)\Big).

Here in the parentheses on the right are the
random part
and
nonrandom part
of the discrepancy. In
[
1
]
it was shown that under (
1.2
),

(1.7)

g(x)\leq ET(0,x)\leq g(x)+C_{14}|x|^{1/2}\log|x|\quad\text{ for all }|x|>1.

In
[
27
]
the error term was improved to
C_{14}(|x|\log|x|)^{1/2}
, and in
[
14
]
to
c_{\eta}|x|^{1/2}(\log|x|)^{\eta}
for all
\eta>0
. For the Euclidean first passage percolation of
[
16
]
, the analog of (
1.7
) was proved in
[
12
]
with an error term of
C_{14}\Psi(|x|)\log^{(k)}|x|
for arbitrary
k\geq 1
, where
\Psi(|x|)
is a scale on which an exponential bound is known (analogous to
\sigma(|x|)
in (
1.3
)) and
\log^{(k)}|x|
is the
k
–times–iterated logarithm. Here we will obtain an essentially optimal bound for the nonrandom part: if
\sigma(\cdot)
satisfies certain regularly and (
1.3
) holds, then a log factor as in the earlier bounds is unnecessary in our context: we have

(1.8)

0\leq ET(0,x)-g(x)\leq C_{15}\sigma(|x|).

There is a strong interdependence among this result, our uniform wandering bounds, and (
1.4
), as discussed in Remark
1.7
.

Analogs of (
1.8
), of (
1.4
), and of of our uniform wandering result, with optimal scale
\sigma(|x|)=\text{var}(T(0,x))^{1/2}\asymp|x|^{1/3}
, are known for certain integrable models of directed last passage percolation (LPP). We note that an exponential bound like (
1.3
), but with centering at the analog of
g(x)
instead of at
ET(0,x)
, shows that (i) (
1.8
) must hold, and (ii) an exponential bound like (
1.3
) must also hold with centering at
g(y-x)
. Such a recentered bound
appears in
[
8
]
(extracted from
[
4
]
) and in
[
21
]
for LPP on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with exponential passage times, in
[
22
]
,
[
23
]
for LPP based on a Poisson process in the unit square, and in
[
10
]
for LPP on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with geometric passage times. An analog of our transverse wandering bound for LPP on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with exponential passage times appears in
[
7
]
. All of these require integral probability methods, which are not available for FPP.

Rather than work on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we will consider isotropic models, built on a random graph
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})}
embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The
dilation
of such an embedded graph is the least
C
such that, for every
x,y\in\mathbb{V}
there is a path from
x
to
y
in
\mathbb{G}
for which the total (Euclidean) length of the edges is at most
C|y-x|
.
We say that such a graph has
bounded dilation
if there exists
C
such that with probability one the dilation of
\mathbb{G}
is at most
C
. For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the
restriction of
\mathbb{G}
to

A
is the graph with vertex set
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{V}_{A}}=\{x\in\mathbb{V}:\langle x,y\rangle\in\mathbb{E}
for some
y\in\mathbb{V}\cap A\}
and edge set
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{E}_{A}}=\{\langle x,y\rangle\in\mathbb{E}:x\in A\}
.

We require that the graph
\mathbb{G}
satisfy the assumptions A1 below, which are somewhat stringent and include bounded dilation, but we will construct an example that works. (We see no need to make the graph as general as possible; we simply need one to know we can work with one that has certain desirable properties.) That example is built roughly as follows. We first construct a point process
\mathbb{V}
to serve as vertices, with
\mathbb{V}
satisfying those parts of A1 which involve only the vertices. To make a graph from
\mathbb{V}
we use the Voronoi diagram, which divides
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into closed polyhedrons
\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}Q_{x}}:x\in\mathbb{V}\}
(called
Voronoi cells
), the interior of the polygon
Q_{x}
consisting of those points which are strictly closer to
x
than to any other point of
\mathbb{V}
. We refer to
x
as the
center point
of
Q_{x}
, and define
\varphi
by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varphi(y)}=x
for
y\in Q_{x}
. To produce the
Delaunay graph
(or
Delaunay triangulation
in
d=2
) one places an edge between each pair
x,y\in\mathbb{V}
for which
Q_{x}
and
Q_{y}
have a face of positive
(d-1)
–volume in common. For
d=2
, it is known that the dilation of the Delaunay graph of any locally finite subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is at most 1.998
[
28
]
, ensuring A1 is fully satisfied, but such bounded dilation for
d\geq 3
is not known. We therefore modify the Delaunay graph by adding certain non-nearest-neighbor edges of uniformly bounded length, by a deterministic local rule, and show that bounded dilation then holds. Here and in what follows, by the
length
of an edge
e=\langle x,y\rangle
we mean the Euclidean distance
|y-x|
, which we denote
|e|
.

Our FPP proofs for isotropic random graphs should adapt to
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, but (we expect) only by assuming two unproven properties of FPP on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
: first, uniform curvature of
\partial\mathfrak{B}_{g}
, and second, a kind of smoothness of the mean as the direction changes:

\sup\Big\{|ET(0,x)-ET(0,y)|:\max(|g(x)-r|,|g(y)-r|)\leq C_{16},|y-x|\leq C_{17}\Delta_{r}\Big\}=O(\sigma_{r})\quad\text{as }r\to\infty.

Results in
[
1
]
show that for lattice FPP, the left side is
O(\sigma_{r}\log r)
. This is (implicitly) improved to
O(\sigma_{r}(\log r)^{1/2})
in
[
27
]
and to
O(\sigma_{r}(\log r)^{\kappa})
for all
\kappa>0
, in
[
14
]
.

Here then are the assumptions
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
must satisfy.

A1. Acceptability of random graphs.

(i)

\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
is isotropic, stationary, and ergodic;

(ii)

Bounded hole size: every open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of radius 1 contains at least one vertex of
\mathbb{V}
;

(iii)

Finite range of dependence: there exists
\beta
such that if
A,B
are Lebesgue–measurable subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
separated by distance
d(A,B)\geq\beta
, then the restrictions of
\mathbb{G}
to
A
and to
B
are independent;

(iv)

Bounded dilation: the dilation of
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
is bounded a.s. (and hence equal to some nonrandom
C_{18}
a.s., by (i));

(v)

Exponential bound for the local density: given
r_{0}>0
there exist
C_{19},C_{20}
such that for all
r>r_{0}
and
a\geq 1
,
P(|\mathbb{V}\cap B_{r}(0)|\geq ar^{d})\leq C_{19}e^{-C_{20}a}
;

We say a random graph with these properties is
acceptable
. We will show that acceptable random graphs exist. By rescaling, we may replace radius 1 in (ii) by any other positive value. Condition (v) can be weakened from exponential to stretched exponential; we use exponential to simplify the exposition.

Conditionally on
\mathbb{G}
we define a collection of i.i.d. nonnegative continuous random variables
\eta=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\eta_{e}},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
. Formally the pair
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\omega}=(\mathbb{V},\eta)
is defined on a probability space
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)}
, with
\mathbb{G}
determined by
\mathbb{V}
.

In contrast to the usual FPP on a true lattice, here we view
\eta_{e}
not as a time but as a speed. We thus define the passage time of a bond
e
to be
\eta_{e}|e|
, and proceed “as usual”: for
x,y\in\mathbb{V}
, a
path

\Gamma
from
x
to
y
is a finite sequence of alternating vertices and edges of
\mathbb{G}
, of the form
\Gamma=(x=x_{0},\langle x_{0},x_{1}\rangle,x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},\langle x_{n-1},x_{n}\rangle,x_{n}=y)
. We may designate a path by specifying only the vertices. The
(path) passage time
of
\Gamma
is

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T(\Gamma)}:=\sum_{e\in\Gamma}\eta_{e}|e|,

and the
passage time
from
x
to
y
is

(1.9)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T(x,y)}:=\inf\{T(\Gamma):\Gamma\text{ is a path from $x$ to $y$ in }\mathbb{G}\}.

More generally, for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define

T(x,y):=T(\varphi(x),\varphi(y)).

For technical convenience we do not require that paths be self-avoiding, but for the moment this is irrelevant because geodesics are always self-avoiding.
For general
x,y
not necessarily in
\mathbb{V}
, we take “a geodesic from
x
to
y
” to mean a geodesic from the center point
\varphi(x)
to
\varphi(y)
. Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\zeta(\lambda)}=Ee^{\lambda\eta_{e}}
. We assume the following.

A2.
\eta_{e}
properties.

(i)

\eta_{e}
is a continuous random variable.

(ii)

There exists
\lambda>0
such that
\zeta(\lambda)<\infty
.

Here (i) guarantees that there is at most one geodesic from
x
to
y
a.s., for each
x,y
.

We do not assume that
\sigma(r)
in (
1.3
) is a power of
r
, but we do require that it “grows like
r^{\chi}
” for some
0<\chi<1
in a sense similar to
[
2
]
, as follows.
We call a nonnegative function
\{\sigma(r)=\sigma_{r},r>0\}

powerlike (with exponent
\chi
)
if there exist
0<{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\chi_{1}}<\chi<{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\chi_{2}}
and constants
C_{i}
such that

(1.10)

\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\log\sigma_{r}}{\log r}=\chi\quad\text{ and for all }s\geq r\geq C_{21},\quad C_{22}\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{\chi_{1}}\leq\frac{\sigma_{s}}{\sigma_{r}}\leq C_{23}\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{\chi_{2}}.

If (
1.10
) holds with
\chi_{2}<1
we say
\sigma(\cdot)
is
sublinearly powerlike
.

The requirement that
\sigma(\cdot)
be sublinearly powerlike is perhaps not as stringent as it first appears, due to Lemma
1.2
below. It implies that if one is only interested in fluctuations at the level of exponents
\chi,\xi
, and one knows uniform the exponential tightness (
1.3
) but not necessarily the powerlike property for
\sigma(\cdot)
, then there is a sublinearly powerlike function with the same
\chi
for which (
1.3
) holds.

Remark 1.1
.

If
\sigma(\cdot)
is powerlike, then so is the increasing function
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\sigma}(r)}=\sup_{s\leq r}\sigma(s)
; by further increasing
\hat{\sigma}
(though by at most a constant factor) we may make it strictly increasing and continuous while preserving the powerlike property. Therefore we may and do without loss of generality always assume
\sigma(\cdot)
is strictly increasing and continuous. The inverse function
\Delta^{-1}
is well-defined, and
for
\xi=(1+\chi)/2\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
we have
\Delta(r)\asymp r^{\xi}
and
\Delta^{-1}(a)\asymp a^{1/\xi}
in the sense that

\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\log\Delta(r)}{\log r}=\xi,\qquad\lim_{a\to\infty}\frac{\log\Delta^{-1}(a)}{\log a}=\frac{1}{\xi}.

The following is proved in section
8
.

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rho}:(1,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
satisfy

\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{\log\rho_{r}}{\log r}=\chi\in(0,\infty).

Then there exist
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{\rho}}\geq\rho
which is sublinearly powerlike with the same exponent
\chi
, and given
\epsilon>0
we may take
\tilde{\rho}
to satisfy (
1.10
) with
|\chi_{i}-\chi|<\epsilon,\ i=1,2
.

For general
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
not necessarily in
\mathbb{V}
, we write
\Gamma_{xy}
for
\Gamma_{\varphi(x),\varphi(y)}
.
In general we view
\Gamma_{xy}
as an undirected path, but at times we will refer to, for example, the first point of
\Gamma_{xy}
with some property. Hence when appropriate, and clear from the context, we view
\Gamma_{xy}
as a path from
\varphi(x)
to
\varphi(y)
.

Our final standard assumption is the following.

A3. Uniform exponential tightness.

For some
\sigma(\cdot)
which is sublinearly powerlike with exponent
\chi\in(0,1)
,

(1.11)

P\Big(|T(x,y)-ET(x,y)|\geq t\sigma(|y-x|)\Big)\leq C_{24}e^{-C_{25}t}\quad\text{for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

The isotropic property assumed for
\mathbb{V}
in A1 means that
g(x)=\mu|x|
for all
x
, where
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mu}=g(e_{1})
, and
ET(0,x)
depends only on
|x|
, so we define

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}h(r)}=ET(0,re_{1}).

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathfrak{B}_{d-1}}
be the Euclidean unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
and define the cylinders

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}G_{r}(K)}=[0,r]\times K\Delta_{r}\mathfrak{B}_{d-1}.

Here is our first main result.

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. Then given
\epsilon>0
there exist constants
C_{i}=C_{i}(\epsilon)
such that for all
r\geq 1,K\geq 1,t\geq C_{26}K^{2}
,

(1.12)

\displaystyle P\Big(\Big|T(x,y)-ET(x,y)\Big|\geq t\sigma_{r}\text{ for some $x,y\in G_{r}(K)$ with }|y-x|\geq\epsilon r\Big)\leq C_{27}e^{-C_{28}t},

and

(1.13)

P\Big(T(x,y)\leq h(|(y-x)_{1}|)-t\sigma_{r}\text{ for some }x,y\in G_{r}(K)\Big)\leq C_{29}e^{-C_{30}t}.

Equation (
1.13
) is weaker than (
1.12
) in the sense that
h(|(y-x)_{1}|)
is (up to a constant) smaller than
ET(x,y)
(see Lemma
3.6
), but stronger in that it isn’t limited to
|y-x|\geq\epsilon r
.

We can split (
1.12
) into upward and downward deviations:

T(x,y)\geq ET(x,y)+t\sigma_{r}\quad\text{and}\quad T(x,y)\leq ET(x,y)-t\sigma_{r}.

Then the downward–deviations part of (
1.12
) is a consequence of (
1.13
) and Proposition
3.3
below, because

x,y\in G_{r}(K),|y-x|\geq\epsilon r\implies\Big||y-x|-|(y-x)_{1}|\Big|\leq C_{31}K^{2}\sigma_{r}

for some
C_{31}(\epsilon)
. In general, if we think of
x
to
y
as an increment a path might make within
G_{r}(K)
in going from the one end to the other in the
e_{1}
direction, then
(y-x)_{1}
measures progress made by that increment in the
e_{1}
direction, so it is a natural normalization of
T(x,y)
in the context of such paths. It is also sufficient for application to the next two theorems.

Remark 1.4
.

For (
1.12
) in Theorem
1.3
we can replace the conditions
K\geq 1,t\geq C_{26}K^{2}
with
K\geq C_{32},t\geq\epsilon K^{2}
. This is because there exists
m
such that
G_{r}(K)
is contained in
C_{32}\epsilon^{-m}
“thin cylinders” (not necessarily oriented parallel to
e_{1}
) of length between
\epsilon r
and
r
and radius
K_{\epsilon}\Delta_{r}=\sqrt{\epsilon/C_{26}}K\Delta_{r}
such that every pair
x,y
as in (
1.12
) is contained in one of these cylinders. Here
C_{32}
does not depend on
K
or
r
. We can apply the theorem to each thin cylinder, since
t\geq C_{26}K_{\epsilon}^{2}
, then sum over the thin cylinders.

We will use Theorem
1.3
together with a coarse-graining scheme in establishing the following.

Theorem 1.5
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. There exists
C_{33}
such that

(1.14)

\mu|x|\leq h(|x|)\leq\mu|x|+C_{33}\sigma(|x|)\quad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

As we have noted, for
|y-x|
of order
r
, if
\Gamma_{xy}
contains a vertex
z
at distance of order
\gg\Delta_{r}
from
\Pi_{xy}
(not too near
x
or
y
), then the associated extra distance
g(z-x)+g(y-z)-g(y-x)
traveled by the geodesic is of order
\gg\Delta_{r}^{2}/r=\sigma_{r}
, and by Theorem
1.5
the same is true for
h
in place of
g
. Since the corresponding passage times satisfy
T(x,z)+T(z,y)-T(x,y)=0
, this means that either

T(x,y)-ET(x,y)\gg\sigma_{r},\quad T(x,z)-ET(x,z)\ll-\sigma_{r},\quad\text{or}\quad T(z,y)-ET(z,y)\ll-\sigma_{r}.

The assumption (
1.11
) says the first of these is unlikely, and Theorems
1.3
and
1.5
can be used to show it is unlikely that there exists a
z
for which the second or third occurs. (Not without complications, though, as we cannot assume
z\in G_{r}
.) This is the idea behind the following. For
r,s>0
define intervals enlarging
[0,r]
:

(1.15)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{I}_{r,s}}=\begin{cases}[-s^{2}\sigma_{r}\log r,r+s^{2}\sigma_{r}\log r]&\text{if }s\leq(C_{34}\log r)^{1/2};\\
[-s^{2}\sigma_{r},r+s^{2}\sigma_{r}]&\text{if }(C_{34}\log r)^{1/2}<s\leq r/\Delta_{r};\\
[-s\Delta_{r},r+s\Delta_{r}]&\text{if }s>r/\Delta_{r},\end{cases}

where
C_{34}
“sufficiently large” will be specified later, and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}G_{r,s}}=\mathcal{I}_{r,s}\times s\Delta_{r}\mathfrak{B}_{d-1}.

For
s>K
we have
G_{r}(K)\subset G_{r,s}
; in this case we may view
G_{r,s}
as being the cylinder
G_{r}(K)
fattened transversally to width
s\Delta_{r}
, and lengthened by an amount which varies with the size of
s
relative to
r
, chosen to be “enough to make wandering of geodesics out the cylinder end at least as unlikely as out the sides.”

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x^{*}}
for
(x_{2},\dots,x_{d})
so
x=(x_{1},x^{*})
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. There exist
C_{i}
such that for all
K\geq C_{35}
,

\displaystyle P\Big(\Gamma_{xy}

\displaystyle\not\subset G_{r,s}\text{ for some $x,y\in G_{r}(K)$ with }|(y-x)^{*}|\leq(y-x)_{1}\Big)

(1.16)

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}C_{36}e^{-C_{37}s^{2}}&\text{for all }C_{38}K\leq s\leq r/\Delta_{r},\\
C_{36}e^{-C_{37}s\Delta_{r}/\sigma(s\Delta_{r})}&\text{for all }s>r/\Delta_{r}.\end{cases}

We include the condition
|(y-x)^{*}|\leq(y-x)_{1}
because we are primarily interested in transverse fluctuations of geodesics out the side of
G_{r,s}
, so we wish to avoid
y-x
oriented in a too–transverse direction.

Remark 1.7
.

The strategy for proving Theorems
1.3
–
1.6
is as follows:

(1)

prove Theorem
1.3
for downward deviations—this is the most difficult part;

(2)

use Theorem
1.3
for downward deviations to prove Theorem
1.6
restricted to a fixed
(x,y)
;

(3)

use Theorem
1.3
for downward deviations and the restricted Theorem
1.6
to prove Theorem
1.5
;

(4)

use Theorem
1.5
to prove Theorem
1.3
for upward deviations;

(5)

use the full Theorem
1.3
to prove the unrestricted Theorem
1.6
.

Remark 1.8
.

In
[
8
]
and
[
6
]
, an alternate strategy was used to prove LPP analogs (in integrable cases) of Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
in
d=2
. Theorem
1.5
was already known for that context—see the comments following (
1.8
). Essentially the strategy for the Theorem
1.3
analog in
[
8
]
is this, when translated to FPP: first the easier upward–deviations half of Theorem
1.3
is proved. For downward deviations, consider the points 0 and
3re_{1}
, and a cylinder
\mathcal{G}_{r}
of radius
\Delta_{r}
with axis from
re_{1}
to
2re_{1}
. Suppose there are (random) vertices
u,v\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
with
u_{1}<v_{1}
for which the passage time is fast:
T(u,v)\leq h(|v-u|)-3t\sigma_{r}
for some large
t
. From the upward–deviations half of Theorem
1.3
, with high probability we have also
T(x,u)\leq h(|u-x|)+t\sigma_{r}
and
T(v,y)\leq h(|y-v|)+t\sigma_{r}
. From this, using that
\sigma_{r}
is proportional to
r^{1/3}
and
h(r)=\mu r+O(\sigma_{r})
, assuming
t
is large enough,

(1.17)

T(x,y)\leq h(|u-x|)+h(|v-u|)+h(|y-v|)-t\sigma_{r}\leq h(|y-x|)-\frac{t}{2}\sigma_{r}.

This has probability exponentially small in
t
, by (
1.11
), hence so does the probability of such
u,v
existing. This strategy does not work for FPP, however, as it requires one to already know Theorem
1.5
to obtain the second inequality in (
1.17
).

2.
Existence of acceptable random graphs

We construct a random graph
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
satisfying A1. We begin by constructing the point process
\mathbb{V}
of vertices.
We start with a “space–time” Poisson process
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{V}_{0}}
(which we view as a random countable set) of density 1 with respect to Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)
.
We say a point
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

appears at time

t
in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
if
(v,t)\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
, and we say
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
empty at time

t-
if no point in
A
appears in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
during
(0,t)
. We then define

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{V}}=\Big\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:

for some
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
v\in B_{1}(x)
,

v
appears at time
t
in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
and

\displaystyle\qquad\text{$B_{1}(x)$ is empty at time $t-$}\Big\}.

In other words, we keep in
\mathbb{V}
the
\mathbb{R}^{d}
coordinate
v
of a point of
\mathbb{V}_{0}
if
v
is the first point to appear in some ball of radius
1
. Then almost surely, for each
v\in\mathbb{V}
there is a unique point
(v,t_{v}^{*})\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
, and we view
t_{v}^{*}
as the time at which
v
appeared in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
. With probability one, every unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
contains a point of
\mathbb{V}
. We call
\mathbb{V}
the
available–space point process
.

For the set
\mathbb{V}
recall that
\{Q_{v},v\in\mathbb{V}\}
denotes the corresponding Voronoi cells.
More generally we write
Q_{x}
for the Voronoi cell containing any
x
(with some arbitrary convention if
x
is on the boundary of multiple cells), and
\varphi(x)
for the unique point of
\mathbb{V}
in
Q_{x}
. When convenient we view
e=\langle x,y\rangle
as the line segment joining
x
and
y
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}B_{r}(x)}
denote the open Euclidean ball of radius
r
about
x
. For
d=2
, the Delaunay graph of
\mathbb{V}
is our graph
\mathbb{G}
. For
d\geq 3
we fix
0<{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}<1
and use

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{E}}=\{\langle x,y\rangle:d(Q_{x},Q_{y})\leq\delta_{\mathbb{G}}\}.

We call
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
the
augmented Delaunay graph
of
\mathbb{V}
. The edges in
\mathbb{E}
which are not in the Delaunay graph are called
augmentation edges
. We write
x\sim y
to denote that
x,y
are adjacent vertices in
\mathbb{G}
, and
x\sim_{\rm Del}y
to denote adjacency in the Delaunay graph of
\mathbb{V}
. If
y\sim_{\rm Del}z
, then for all
u\in Q_{y}\cap Q_{z},B_{|y-z|/2}(u)\cap\mathbb{V}=\emptyset
. Hence by A1(ii),

(2.1)

|z-y|<2\text{ whenever $y\sim_{\rm Del}z$}.

Similarly if
z\in Q_{y}
then
B_{|z-y|}(z)
contains no point of
\mathbb{V}
, so
|z-y|<1
; thus

(2.2)

Q_{y}\subset B_{1}(y).

Remark 2.1
.

The purpose of augmentation is roughly the following. Consider the line segment
\Pi_{xy}
between
x,y\in\mathbb{V}
. It passes through a sequence of Voronoi cells
Q_{x}=Q_{x_{0}},Q_{x_{1}},\dots,Q_{x_{m}}=Q_{y}
, and there is a corresponding path
x=x_{0}\to x_{1}\to\cdots\to x_{m}=y
in the Delaunay graph. If too many of the cells
Q_{x_{j}}
are “thin,” then the Delaunay path length
\sum_{j=1}^{m}|x_{j}-x_{j-1}|
may be much greater than
|y-x|
, making it difficult to bound the dilation. The augmentation effectively allows paths in
\mathbb{G}
that skip over such problematic sequences of cells, at least for a small distance, enabling us to prove bounded dilation while preserving other properties of the Delaunay triangulation. We can reduce the occurrence of augmentation to involve an arbitrarily small proportion of vertices by using a small enough
\delta>0
. We will not give details here.

For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r>0
let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}A^{r}}=\{x:d(x,A)<r\}
.

Proposition 2.2
.

The augmented Delaunay graph of the available–space point process satisfies A1.

Proof.

A1(i) for
\mathbb{V}
follows from the same properties for
\mathbb{V}_{0}
; since
\mathbb{G}
is constructed from
\mathbb{V}
via isotropic and translation-invariant local rules, A1(i) also holds for
\mathbb{G}
. A1(ii) follows from the fact that the first point of
\mathbb{V}_{0}
to appear in any radius-1 ball is always a point of
\mathbb{V}
.

To prove A1(iii), observe first that by (
2.1
), given
x\in\mathbb{V}
the cell
Q_{x}
and all Delaunay edges
\langle x,y\rangle
are determined by
\mathbb{V}\cap B_{2}(x)
. Therefore the collection of Voronoi cells intersecting
B_{3}(x)
is determined by
\mathbb{V}\cap B_{5}(x)
, and hence so are all augmentation edges
\langle x,y\rangle
.
\mathbb{V}\cap B_{5}(x)
, in turn, is determined by
\mathbb{V}_{0}\cap(B_{7}(x)\times(0,\infty))
, so for
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the restriction
(\mathbb{V}_{A},\mathbb{E}_{A})
is determined by
\mathbb{V}_{0}\cap(A^{7}\times(0,\infty))
. Since
\mathbb{V}_{0}
is independent in disjoint regions, A1(iii) follows with
\beta=14
.

Turning to A1(v), let
q=1/(1+\lfloor 3\sqrt{d}\rfloor)
and
r>r_{0}>0
, and let
r_{1}=r_{0}\wedge 1
. For
x\in qr_{1}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}J_{x}}
denote the cube
\prod_{i=1}^{d}[x_{i},x_{i}+qr_{1})
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{J}_{x}}=\{J_{y}:y\in qr_{1}\mathbb{Z}^{d},|y-x|_{\infty}=2qr_{1}\}
. This means the
5^{d}-3^{d}
cubes in
\mathcal{J}_{x}
form a shell around
J_{x}
, with a smaller shell of cubes in between, and the diameter of
\mathcal{J}_{x}
is less than
2r_{1}
. Then any radius-
1
ball intersecting
J_{x}
must contain a cube in
\mathcal{J}_{x}
. Letting
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}t^{*}(J_{x})}=\max\{t_{v}^{*}:v\in\mathbb{V}\cap J_{x}\}
it follows that at time
t^{*}(J_{x})
, for some
J_{y}\in\mathcal{J}_{x}
, at least
|\mathbb{V}\cap J_{x}|
points of
\mathbb{V}_{0}
have appeared in
J_{x}
but none in
J_{y}
. Letting
N_{xy}
be the number of points of
\mathbb{V}_{0}
appearing in
J_{x}
before the first point of
\mathbb{V}_{0}
appears in
J_{y}
, this says that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}N_{x}}:=\max_{J_{y}\in\mathcal{J}_{x}}N_{xy}\geq|\mathbb{V}\cap J_{x}|
. Since
|\mathcal{J}_{x}|\leq 5^{d}
it follows that

(2.3)

\displaystyle P(|\mathbb{V}\cap J_{x}|\geq n)

\displaystyle\leq P(N_{x}\geq n)\leq\sum_{J_{y}\in\mathcal{J}_{x}}P(N_{xy}\geq n)\leq 5^{d}2^{-n},

and hence for
\lambda<\log 2
,

(2.4)

Ee^{\lambda N_{x}}\leq\frac{5^{d}}{1-\frac{e^{\lambda}}{2}}.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{I}_{0}}=\{J_{x}:J_{x}\cap[-r,r)^{d}\neq\emptyset\}
, so
|\mathcal{I}_{0}|\leq(2(1+\frac{r}{qr_{1}}))^{d}
and
B_{r}(0)\subset\cup_{J_{x}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}}\,J_{x}
. Now
\mathcal{I}_{0}
is a lattice of cubes, and we divide it into
5^{d}
sublattices, each consisting of a cube
J_{x}
together with all its translates by vectors in
5qr_{1}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
which intersect
[-r,r)^{d}
. We label these sublattices of cubes as
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{I}_{0,1},\dots,\mathcal{I}_{0,5^{d}}}
, and the cardinality satisfies

(2.5)

|\mathcal{I}_{0,j}|\leq\left(2\left(1+\frac{r}{5qr_{1}}\right)\right)^{d}

for each
j
. We denote the corresponding union of cubes as
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}I_{0,j}}=\cup_{J_{x}\in\mathcal{I}_{0,j}}J_{x}
. The spacing of the cubes in
\mathcal{I}_{0,j}
means that that the shells
\{\mathcal{J}_{x}:J_{x}\in\mathcal{I}_{0,j}\}
are disjoint, so the variables
\{N_{x}:J_{x}\in\mathcal{I}_{0,j}\}
are i.i.d.

For
a\geq 1
, taking
\lambda=1/5
in (
2.4
) we obtain
Ee^{\lambda N_{x}}\leq 3\cdot 5^{d}
and hence using (
2.5
), provided
a
is large (depending on
r_{1}
),

\displaystyle P(|\mathbb{V}\cap B_{r}(0)|\geq ar^{d})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{5^{d}}P\left(|\mathbb{V}\cap I_{0,j}|\geq\frac{ar^{d}}{5^{d}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{5^{d}}P\left(\sum_{J_{x}\in\mathcal{I}_{0,j}}N_{x}\geq\frac{ar^{d}}{5^{d}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 5^{d}\left(3\cdot 5^{d}\right)^{|\mathcal{I}_{0,j}|}e^{-ar^{d}/5^{d+1}}

(2.6)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-ar^{d}/2\cdot 5^{d+1}},

proving A1(v).

Finally we consider A1(iv). Let
x,y\in\mathbb{V}
. As in Remark
2.1
,
\Pi_{xy}
passes through a sequence of Voronoi cells
Q_{x}=Q_{x_{0}},Q_{x_{1}},\dots,Q_{x_{m}}=Q_{y}
, and there is a corresponding path
x=x_{0}\to x_{1}\to\cdots\to x_{m}=y
in the Delaunay graph. (There is probability 0 that
\Pi_{xy}
intersects some
Q_{u}
in just a single point, so we will ignore this possibility, meaning that “passes through” here is unambiguous.) For
j<m
let
a_{j}
be the first point of
Q_{x_{j}}
in
\Pi_{xy}
and let
a_{m}=y
, so by convexity of cells,
Q_{x_{j}}\cap\Pi_{xy}=[a_{j},a_{j+1}]
for all
0\leq j<m
. We select indices
0=j(0)<j(1)<\cdots<j(\ell)=m
iteratively, taking
j(k+1)
as the least index
j>j(k)
for which either
|a_{j+1}-a_{j(k)+1}|>\delta_{\mathbb{G}}
or
j=m
. Then
\langle x_{j(k)},x_{j(k+1)}\rangle
is always a Delaunay or augmentation edge, so we consider the path
x=x_{j(0)}\to x_{j(1)}\to\cdots\to x_{j(\ell)}=y
in
\mathbb{G}
; in particular we want to bound
|x_{j(k+1)}-x_{j(k)}|
relative to
|a_{j(k+1)+1}-a_{j(k)+1}|
for
0\leq k\leq\ell-2
.

For
k\leq\ell-1
we have using (
2.2
)

|x_{j(k+1)}-x_{j(k)}|\leq|x_{j(k+1)}-a_{j(k+1)}|+|a_{j(k+1)}-a_{j(k)+1}|+|a_{j(k)+1}-x_{j(k)}|\leq 2+\delta_{\mathbb{G}}

so for
k\leq\ell-2
,

|a_{j(k+1)+1}-a_{j(k)+1}|>\delta_{\mathbb{G}}\geq\frac{\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}{2+\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}|x_{j(k+1)}-x_{j(k)}|.

Therefore if
\ell\geq 2
,

(2.7)

\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-2}|x_{j(k+1)}-x_{j(k)}|\leq\frac{2+\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}{\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-2}|a_{j(k+1)+1}-a_{j(k)+1}|=\frac{2+\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}{\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}|a_{j(\ell-1)-1}-a_{1}|\leq\frac{2+\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}{\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}|y-x|.

Having
\ell\geq 2
also ensures
|y-x|\geq|a_{j(1)}-a_{j(0)}|>\delta_{\mathbb{G}}
so

|x_{j(\ell)}-x_{j(\ell-1)}|=|y-x_{j(\ell-1)}|\leq|y-a_{j(\ell-1)+1}|+|a_{j(\ell-1)+1}-x_{j(\ell-1)}|\leq|y-x|+1\leq\frac{1+\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}{\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}|y-x|

which with (
2.7
) yields

(2.8)

\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}|x_{j(k+1)}-x_{j(k)}|\leq\frac{3+2\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}{\delta_{\mathbb{G}}}|y-x|.

On the other hand, if
\ell=1
then

\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}|x_{j(k+1)}-x_{j(k)}|=|y-x|

so (
2.8
) still holds. This proves bounded dilation.
∎

3.
Straightness of geodesics and and regularity of means

For
q>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let

(3.1)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\psi_{q}(x)}=\text{ the point of $q\mathbb{Z}^{d}$ closest to $x$ (with ties broken arbitrarily)},\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}F_{y}}=\psi_{q}^{-1}(y),y\in q\mathbb{Z}^{d},

the latter being a cube (ignoring the boundary.) More generally for
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define
F_{u}
to be the cube
F_{y}
containing
u
, with some arbitrary rule for cube–boundary points.

The bound (
1.3
) applies to deterministic
x,y
; we cannot for example take
x,y\in\mathbb{V}
. Instead for random
x,y
we can apply (
1.3
) to nearby points of
q\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for some
q
, and use the following. It is the only place the assumption A2(iv) of bounded dilation is used.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. There exist constants
C_{i}
as follows.

(i) Let
r\geq 2
and
t\geq C_{39}\log r
. Then

P(\text{there exist $x,y\in B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{V}$ with }T(x,y)\geq tr)\leq C_{40}e^{-C_{41}t}.

(ii) For all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

ET(x,y)\leq C_{42}(|y-x|\vee 1).

Proof.

To prove (i) we condition on
\mathbb{V}
. Given
x,y\in B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{V}
, by A1(iv) there exists a path
x=x_{0},x_{1},..,x_{m}=y
in
\mathbb{G}
with

\sum_{j=1}^{m}|x_{j}-x_{j-1}|\leq C_{18}|y-x|\leq 2C_{18}r.

Writing
\eta_{j}
for
\eta_{\langle x_{j-1}x_{j}\rangle}
, for
\lambda>0
we have

\displaystyle P\Big(T(x,y)\geq t|y-x|\,\Big|\,\mathbb{V}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}|x_{j}-x_{j-1}|\eta_{j}\geq t|y-x|\,\Bigg|\,\mathbb{V}\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\lambda t|y-x|}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\zeta\big(\lambda|x_{j}-x_{j-1}|\big)

(3.2)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\lambda t|y-x|}\zeta\big(C_{18}\lambda|y-x|\big),

so using A2(ii) it follows that for some
c_{i}
,

(3.3)

P\Big(T(x,y)\geq t|y-x|\,\Big|\,\mathbb{V}\Big)\leq e^{-I_{\eta}(t/C_{18})}\leq c_{0}e^{-c_{1}t},

where
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}I_{\eta}(t)}=\sup_{\gamma>0}(\gamma t-\log\zeta(\gamma))
is the large-deviations rate function of the variables
\eta_{e}
.
From this, using A1(v),

\displaystyle P(\text{there exist $x,y\in B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{V}$ with }T(x,y)\geq tr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}P(|B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{V}|=n){n\choose 2}c_{1}e^{-c_{1}t/2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}E|B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{V}|^{2}e^{-c_{1}t/2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}r^{4}e^{-c_{2}t/2}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}e^{-c_{3}t},

proving (i).

To prove (ii) we apply (
3.3
) to
\varphi(x)
and
\varphi(y)
, and use the fact that
|\varphi(x)-x|\leq 1
for all
x
.
∎

Building on Lemma
3.1
we obtain the following.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. There exist constants
C_{i}
as follows. For all
r\geq 2,u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t>0
with
\sigma(|u-v|)\geq r
and
t\sigma(|u-v|)\geq C_{43}r\log r
,

\displaystyle P\Big(

\displaystyle\text{there exist $x\in B_{r}(u)\cap\mathbb{V},y\in B_{r}(v)\cap\mathbb{V}$ with }|T(x,y)-ET(u,v)|\geq t\sigma(|u-v|)\Big)

(3.5)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{44}e^{-C_{45}t}.

Proof.

Using Lemma
3.1
(i) and (
1.3
) we have

\displaystyle P\Big(

\displaystyle\text{there exist $x\in B_{r}(u)\cap\mathbb{V},y\in B_{r}(v)\cap\mathbb{V}$ with }|T(x,y)-ET(u,v)|\geq t\sigma(|u-v|)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq P\Big(\text{there exists $x\in B_{r}(u)\cap\mathbb{V}$ with }T(x,u)\geq t\sigma(|u-v|)/3\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+P\Big(\text{there exists $y\in B_{r}(v)\cap\mathbb{V}$ with }T(y,v)\geq t\sigma(|u-v|)/3\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+P\Big(|T(u,v)-ET(u,v)|\geq t\sigma(|u-v|)/3\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2C_{40}e^{-C_{41}t\sigma(|u-v|)/3r}+C_{24}e^{-C_{25}t/3}

(3.6)

\displaystyle\quad\leq c_{0}e^{-c_{1}t}.

∎

We write

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}x\to y_{1}\to\cdots\to y_{k}\to y}\quad\text{in }\Gamma_{xy}

to denote that the vertices
y_{i}\in\mathbb{V}
appear in the order
y_{1},\dots,y_{k}
in traversing
\Gamma_{xy}
from
x
to
y
. For
a
preceding
b
in
\Gamma_{xy}
we write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Gamma_{xy}[a,b]}
for the segment of
\Gamma_{xy}
from
a
to
b
. (Here we do not require
a,b\in\mathbb{V}
.) For
v
in a geodesic
\Gamma_{xy}
and
0<s<|v-x|
, let
u
be
the first vertex in
\Gamma_{xy}\cap\mathbb{V}
before
v
satisfying
\Gamma_{uv}\subset B_{s}(v)
. We then call
\Gamma_{uv}
the
trailing s-segment
of
v
in
\Gamma_{xy}
. Note that by (
2.1
),
s\geq|u-v|\geq s-2
. Define the hyperplanes, slabs, and halfspaces

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}H_{s}}=\{(x_{1},x^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x_{1}=s\},\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}H_{[r,s]}}=\{(x_{1},x^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:r\leq x_{1}\leq s\},

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}H_{s}^{+}}=\{(x_{1},x^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x_{1}\geq s\},\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}H_{s}^{-}}=\{(x_{1},x^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x_{1}\leq s\}.

We turn next to a weaker version of Theorem
1.5
similar to (
1.7
); we will need it on the way to the proof of Theorem
1.5
. The proof of (
1.7
) in
[
1
]
does not carry over immediately to the present situation, as it uses translation invariance of the lattice. But
we can use radial symmetry here to give a distinctly shorter proof than that of (
1.7
) in
[
1
]
.

Recall
h(r)-\mu r
is nonnegative by subadditivity of
h
. For technical convenience we assume
\beta>4
in A1(ii).

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3.
There exists
C_{46}
such that for all
r\geq 2
,

(3.7)

\mu r\leq h(r)\leq\mu r+C_{46}\sigma_{r}\log r.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the bound for all sufficiently large
r
, so we will tacitly assume
r
is large, as needed.

We consider the geodesic
\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}}
for a fixed large
n
. Let
\beta
be as in A1(iii). For
0\leq j\leq n-1
let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}v_{j}}
be the first vertex
v
in
\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}}\cap\mathbb{V}
with the property that the trailing
(r-2\beta)
-segment of
v
in
\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}}
is contained in
H_{jr}^{+}
, and let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}u_{j}}

be the starting point of this trailing segment. From (
2.1
) it is easy to see that we must have
jr\leq(u_{j})_{1}\leq jr+2
. It follows that
\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}}[u_{j},v_{j}]\subset B_{r-2\beta}(v_{j})\cap H_{jr}^{+}\cap H_{(j+1)r-2\beta+2}^{-}
; we denote this last region by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}W_{v_{j}}}
. Note that any two sets
W_{v_{j}}
are separated by distance at least
2\beta-2
.

We need to control the entropy of the collection of pairs
\{(u_{j},v_{j}):0\leq j<n\}
. To do this, we enlarge this collection in such a way that we can put a natural tree structure on it. To that end, let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}v_{n}}
be the first vertex in
\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}}\cap\mathbb{V}
, if one exists, which is at distance at least
r
from
\cup_{0\leq j<n}W_{v_{j}}
. Then let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}u_{n}}\in\mathbb{V}
be such that
\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}}[u_{n},v_{n}]
is the trailing
(r-2\beta)
-segment of
v_{n}
in
\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}}
, and let
W_{v_{n}}=B_{r-2\beta}(v_{n})
, which contains
\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}}[u_{n},v_{n}]
. We repeat this to obtain
(u_{n+1},v_{n+1}),\dots,(u_{N},v_{N})
, stopping when no
v_{N+1}
exists. Note this preserves the property of separation by distance at least
2\beta-2
. Also, each new
v_{j}
is within distance
2r-2\beta+2
of some already-existing
v_{i}
.

We define the discrete approximations
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{u}_{j}}=\psi_{1/\sqrt{d}}(u_{j}),{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{v}_{j}}=\psi_{1/\sqrt{d}}(v_{j})
, and then let

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{W}_{j}}=\begin{cases}B_{r-2\beta+1}(\hat{v}_{j})\cap H_{jr-1}^{+}\cap H_{(j+1)r-2\beta+3}^{-}&\text{if }0\leq j<n,\\
B_{r-2\beta+1}(\hat{v}_{j})&\text{if }n\leq j\leq N.\end{cases}

which contains
W_{v_{j}}
; the sets
\hat{W}_{j},0\leq j\leq N
are separated from each other by distance at least
2\beta-6
. We call
\{(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j}):0\leq j<n\}

primary pairs
, and
\{(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j}):n\leq j\leq N\}

secondary pairs
. Since
r-2\beta\geq|u_{j}-v_{j}|\geq r-2\beta-2
, we have

(3.8)

r>r-2\beta+2\geq|\hat{u}_{j}-\hat{v}_{j}|\geq r-2\beta-4>r-3\beta.

We now make a graph with vertices
\{(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j}):0\leq j\leq N\}
by placing an edge between the ith and jth pairs if
|\hat{v}_{j}-\hat{v}_{i}|\leq 4r
. The construction ensures that the resulting graph is connected, and it is easy to see that the disjointness of the sets
\hat{W}_{j}
means the number of neighbors of any pair is bounded by some
c_{0}
. We label
(u_{0},v_{0})
as the root, and by some arbitrary algorithm, we take a spanning tree of the graph, which we denote
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}})}
. For counting purposes, we view two such trees as the same if they have the same pairs
\{(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j}):0\leq j\leq N\}
, and the same set of primary pairs. We define
parents
and
offspring
in this rooted tree in the usual way: for a given pair
(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j})
, its parent is the first pair after
(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j})
in the unique path from
(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j})
to the root, and its offspring are those pairs having
(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j})
as parent.

The tree
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}})
determines what we will call an abstract tree, in which all that is specified is the number of offspring of the root, then the number of offspring of each of these offspring, etc. The number of possible abstract trees here with
N+1
vertices is at most
c_{0}^{N}
, and, provided
r
is large, for each such abstract tree the number of corresponding actual trees
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}})
is at most
((8r)^{d})^{2N+2}
, so the number of trees
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}})
consisting of
N+1
pairs is at most
(c_{1}r)^{2d(N+1)}
.

We now consider all possible trees
\mathcal{T}_{0}
, with vertices
\{(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j}):0\leq j\leq N\}
, and with
\{(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j}):0\leq j<n\}
primary. Let
v(\mathcal{T}_{0})
denote the number of vertices in the tree
\mathcal{T}_{0}
. We have

\displaystyle P\Big(T(0,nre_{1})\leq n(\mu r

\displaystyle+1)\Big)=\sum_{N\geq n}\,\sum_{\mathcal{T}_{0}:v(\mathcal{T}_{0})=N}P\Big(T(0,nre_{1})\leq n(\mu r+1),\mathcal{T}(\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}})=\mathcal{T}_{0}\Big)

(3.9)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{N\geq n}(c_{1}r)^{2d(N+1)}\max_{\mathcal{T}_{0}:v(\mathcal{T}_{0})=N}P\Big(T(0,nre_{1})\leq n(\mu r+1),\mathcal{T}(\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}})=\mathcal{T}_{0}\Big)

We proceed by contradiction: we want to show that for some
C_{46}
, if
h(r)>\mu r+C_{46}\sigma_{r}\log r
then the right side of (
3
) approaches 0 as
n\to\infty
, which means
\limsup_{n}T(0,nre_{1})/n\geq\mu r+1
a.s. , contradicting the definition of
\mu
.

Thus suppose

(3.10)

h(r)>\mu r+2C_{46}\sigma_{r}\log r,

with
C_{46}
to be specified, and fix
\mathcal{T}_{0}
with vertices
\{(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j}):0\leq j\leq N\}
with
N\geq n
. For
u,v\in\mathbb{V}\cap\hat{W}_{j}
let

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T_{\hat{W}_{j}}(u,v)}=\inf\{T(\Gamma):\Gamma\text{ is a path from $u$ to $v$ in }\mathbb{G},\Gamma\subset\hat{W}_{j}\},

taking the value
\infty
when there is no such path.

T_{\hat{W}_{j}}(u,v)
is determined by the configuration in

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{W}_{j}^{1}}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:d(u,\hat{W}_{j})<1\}.

There must exist
u_{j},v_{j}\in\hat{W}_{j}\cap\mathbb{V}
satisfying
|u_{j}-\hat{u}_{j}|\leq 1,|v_{j}-\hat{v}_{j}|\leq 1,\Gamma_{u_{j},v_{j}}\subset\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}}\cap\hat{W}_{j}
and

\sum_{j=0}^{N}T_{\hat{W}_{j}}(u_{j},v_{j})\leq T(0,nre_{1}).

It follows that, letting

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{T}_{j}}=\inf\{T_{\hat{W}_{j}}(u_{j},v_{j}):u_{j},v_{j}\in\mathbb{V},|u_{j}-\hat{u}_{j}|\leq 1,|v_{j}-\hat{v}_{j}|\leq 1\},

the
\hat{T}_{j}
’s are independent (since the sets
\hat{W}_{j}^{1}
are separated from each other by distance more than
2\beta-8>\beta
) and satisfy

(3.11)

\sum_{j=0}^{N}\hat{T}_{j}\leq T(0,nre_{1}).

Each
\hat{T}_{j}
is stochastically larger than

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T_{j}}=\inf\{T(u_{j},v_{j}):u_{j},v_{j}\in\mathbb{V},|u_{j}-\hat{u}_{j}|\leq 1,|v_{j}-\hat{v}_{j}|\leq 1\}

From (
1.10
) and (
3.8
) we have for some
c_{2}
that
\sigma(|\hat{u}_{j}-\hat{v}_{j}|)\leq c_{2}\sigma_{r}.
From (
3.8
), (
3.10
), and subadditivity we also have

ET(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j})\geq h(r)-h(r-|\hat{u}_{j}-\hat{v}_{j}|)\geq h(r)-c_{3}\geq\mu r+2C_{46}\sigma_{r}\log r-c_{3}\geq\mu r+C_{46}\sigma_{r}\log r.

Hence from Lemma
3.2
we obtain that provided
r
is large, for all
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle P\Big(T_{j}\leq\mu r+C_{46}\sigma_{r}\log r-t\sigma_{r}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq P\Big(T_{j}\leq ET(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j})-t\sigma_{r}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq P\Big(T_{j}\leq ET(\hat{u}_{j},\hat{v}_{j})-c_{2}^{-1}t\sigma(|\hat{u}_{j}-\hat{v}_{j}|)\Big)

(3.12)

\displaystyle\leq C_{44}e^{-C_{45}t/c_{2}}.

By increasing
C_{44}
we may make this valid for all
t>0
. We then have for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lambda}=C_{45}/2c_{2}\sigma_{r}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}M}=\mu r+C_{46}\sigma_{r}\log r
,

\displaystyle Ee^{-\lambda\hat{T}_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq Ee^{-\lambda T_{j}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}P(T_{j}\leq x)\lambda e^{-\lambda x}\ dx

\displaystyle=e^{-\lambda M}\int_{-\infty}^{M/\sigma_{r}}P(T_{j}\leq M-t\sigma_{r})\lambda\sigma_{r}e^{\lambda\sigma_{r}t}\ dt

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\lambda M}\left[\int_{-\infty}^{0}\lambda\sigma_{r}e^{\lambda\sigma_{r}t}\ dt+\int_{0}^{M/\sigma_{r}}C_{44}e^{-C_{45}t/c_{2}}\lambda\sigma_{r}e^{\lambda\sigma_{r}t}\ dt\right]

(3.13)

\displaystyle\leq(1+C_{44})e^{-\lambda M}.

Recalling (
3
) and (
3.11
) we then have

\displaystyle P\Big(T(0,nre_{1})\leq n(\mu r+1),\mathcal{T}(\Gamma_{0,nre_{1}})=\mathcal{T}_{0}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\hat{T}_{j}\leq n(\mu r+1)\right)

(3.14)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\lambda(\mu r+1)n}(1+C_{44})^{N+1}e^{-\lambda M(N+1)},

so by (
3
), provided
r
(and hence
\lambda M
) and
C_{46}
(in (
3.10
)) are large,

\displaystyle P\Big(T(0,nre_{1})\leq n(\mu r+1)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{N\geq n}(c_{1}r)^{2d(N+1)}e^{\lambda(\mu r+1)n}(1+C_{44})^{N+1}e^{-\lambda M(N+1)}

\displaystyle\leq 2(c_{1}r)^{2d(n+1)}e^{\lambda(\mu r+1)n}(1+C_{44})^{n+1}e^{-\lambda M(n+1)}

(3.15)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-n\log r}.

As we have noted, since this approaches 0 as
n\to\infty
, it contradicts the fact that
T(0,nre_{1})/n\to\mu r
a.s. Thus (
3.10
) must be false.
∎

We need to use a result from
[
2
]
to the effect that “geodesics are very straight.” It is proved there for FPP on a lattice, but the proof goes through essentially unchanged for the present context. The heuristics are as follows: suppose the geodesic
\Gamma_{0,re_{1}}
passes through a vertex
u=(u_{1},u^{*})
at distance
s
from
\Pi_{0,re_{1}}
; by symmetry we may suppose
u\in H_{r/2}^{-}
. The geodesic then travels a corresponding extra distance
g(u)+g(re_{1}-u)-g(re_{1})
. If the angle between
u
and
re_{1}
is small, this extra distance is of order
|u^{*}|^{2}/u_{1}
, and from (
1.11
), the cost of this (meaning log of the probability) is of order
|u^{*}|^{2}/u_{1}\sigma(u_{1})
. If instead the angle between
u
and
re_{1}
is not small, the extra distance is of order
|u|
and the cost is of order
|u|/\sigma(|u|)
. We can combine these into a single statement by saying the cost for general
u
should be whichever of these two costs is smaller, at least for
u\in H_{[0,r]}
.

The exact formulation of the straightness result contains extra log factors relative to the preceding heuristic, due to the need to bound the probability for all
u
simultaneously. It is as follows, using the constants
C_{i},\chi_{i}
of (
1.10
). Define
\sigma^{*}(s)
and
\Phi(s)
by

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Phi(s)}=\frac{s}{C_{23}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma^{*}(s)}\log(2+s)}=\frac{s^{\chi_{2}}}{C_{23}}\sup_{t\leq s}\frac{t^{1-\chi_{2}}}{\sigma_{t}\log(2+t)}.

Here factoring out a power of
s
on the right, and the use of the sup, ensure that
\Phi
is strictly increasing. Note that by (
1.10
) we have

(3.16)

C_{23}^{-1}\sigma_{s}\leq\sigma^{*}(s)\leq\sigma_{s}.

Then define

(3.17)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Xi(s)}=(s\sigma(s)\log(2+s))^{1/2},\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}D(u)}=\begin{cases}\min\left(\frac{|u^{*}|^{2}}{\Xi(u_{1})^{2}},\Phi\left(\max\big(|u_{1}|,|u^{*}|\big)\right)\right)&\text{if }u_{1}\geq 0,\\
\Phi\left(\max\big(|u_{1}|,|u^{*}|\big)\right)&\text{if }u_{1}<0.\end{cases}

Note that by (
1.10
) and (
3.16
), for large
s
,

(3.18)

\frac{1}{C_{23}\Phi(s)}\leq\left(\frac{\Xi(s)}{s}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\Phi(s)}.

The “min” in the definition of
D
is in accordance with our heuristic: from
[
2
]
, we have for large
|u|
that for
C_{23}
from (
1.10
),

(3.19)

D(u)=\begin{cases}\Phi\left(\max\big(|u_{1}|,|u^{*}|\big)\right)&\text{if }|u^{*}|\geq u_{1},\\
\frac{|u^{*}|^{2}}{\Xi(u_{1})^{2}}&\text{if }|u^{*}|\leq C_{23}^{-1/2}u_{1}.\end{cases}

Finally, define the symmetric version of
D
:

(3.20)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}D_{r}(u)}=\begin{cases}D(u)&\text{if }u_{1}\leq\frac{r}{2}\\
D(re_{1}-u)&\text{if }u_{1}>\frac{r}{2}.\end{cases}

This makes the right half of the region
\{u:D_{r}(u)\leq c\}
the mirror image of the left half; this region is a “tube” (narrower near the ends) surrounding the line from 0 to
re_{1}
bounded by the shell
\{u:|u^{*}|=c^{1/2}\Xi(u_{1})\}
, augmented by a cylinder of radius
\Phi^{-1}(c)
and length
2\Phi^{-1}(c)
around each endpoint, so we will call it a
tube–and–cylinders region
.

We will also consider tube–and–cylinders regions around general pairs
u,v
in place of
0,re_{1}
. To that end, let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Theta_{uv}}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be translation by
-u
followed by some unitary transformation which takes
v-u
to the positive horizontal axis, so that
\Theta_{uv}(u)=0,\Theta_{uv}(v)=|v-u|e_{1}
. (The particular choice of unitary transformation does not matter.) Then
\Theta_{uv}^{-1}(\{w:D_{|v-u|}(w)\leq c\})
is a tube–and–cylinders region containing
\Pi_{uv}
.

The proof of the acceptable–random–graphs version of the straightness bound is little changed from the lattice-FPP version in
[
2
]
; we can readily use Lemma
3.1
(i) to change the result from “point–to–point” (say, 0 to
re_{1}
) to “ball–to–ball,” with a sup over
x
and
y
. We omit the details.

Proposition 3.4
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. There exist constants
C_{i}
as follows. For all
r,t>0
,

(3.21)

P\left(\sup_{x\in B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{V},\ y\in B_{1}(re_{1})\cap\mathbb{V}}\ \sup_{u\in\Gamma_{xy}}D_{r}(u)\geq t\right)\leq C_{47}e^{-C_{48}t\log t}.

We next bound transverse increments of passage times from 0, that is, increments which are (approximately) along the boundary of a ball of large radius
r
, over distances
\ll\Delta_{r}
.
The following lattice-FPP result from
[
2
]
carries over straightforwardly to the present context with the help of Lemma
3.1
(i), and again we omit details. Recalling the cubes
F_{u}
of side
q
define

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{T}(u,v)}=\min\{T(y,z):y\in F_{u},z\in F_{v}\},\quad u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Proposition 3.5
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. There exist constants
C_{i}
as follows. For all
u,v\in q\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with

(3.22)

|u|\geq C_{49},\quad|g(u)-g(v)|\leq C_{50},\quad\text{and}\quad 3\leq|u-v|\leq C_{51}\Delta(|u|),

and all
\lambda\geq C_{52}
, we have

(3.23)

P\Big(\hat{T}(v,0)-\hat{T}(u,0)\geq\lambda\sigma(\Delta^{-1}(|u-v|))\log|u-v|\Big)\leq C_{53}e^{-C_{54}\lambda\log|u-v|}.

We next prove a seemingly obvious fact:
h(r)=ET(0,re_{1})
is approximately increasing in
r
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. Given
0<\epsilon<1
there exist constants
C_{i}
as follows.

(i) Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}q}>(1+\chi)/(1-\chi)
. Let
r,s>0
satisfying
\min(r,s)\geq C_{55}\epsilon^{-q}
. Then

(3.24)

h(r+s)\geq h(r)+(1-\epsilon)h(s).

Here
C_{55}=C_{55}(q)
.

(ii) For all
r,s\geq 0
,

(3.25)

h(r+s)\geq h(r)+(1-\epsilon)h(s)-C_{56}.

Remark 3.7
.

Lemma
3.6
(i) can be reformulated as follows: given
\gamma>\chi/\xi
there exist
C_{i}(\gamma)
such that

h(r+s)\geq h(r)+h(s)-C_{57}s^{\gamma}\quad\text{for all }r\geq s\geq C_{58}.

Also, from Proposition
3.3
we have for some
C_{59}

h(r+s)\geq\mu(r+s)\geq h(r)+h(s)-2C_{46}\sigma_{r}\log r\quad\text{for all }r\geq s\geq 2,

but when
s\leq\sigma_{r}\log r
this does not yield (
3.24
).

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

We prove (i), then obtain (ii) as a straightforward consequence. The idea is to show that
\Gamma_{0,(r+s)e_{1}}
must with high probability approach
(r+s)e_{1}
approximately horizontally, which forces
T(0,(r+s)e_{1})-T(0,re_{1})
to be near
h(s)
with high probability; a non-horizontal approach would force the sup in (
3.21
) to be large.

Suppose (
3.24
) holds under the added condition
s\leq r/4
. Then for
s>r/4
we can take
n
with
s/n\leq r/4<s/(n-1)
and see that the hypotheses are satisfied with
s/n
in place of
s
. (This may require increasing
C_{55}
, but without dependence on
n
.) Applying (
3.24
)
n
times then yields

h(r+s)=h\left(r+n\frac{s}{n}\right)\geq h(r)+n(1-\epsilon)h\left(\frac{s}{n}\right)\geq h(r)+(1-\epsilon)h(s).

Therefore it is sufficient to prove the lemma for
s\leq r/4
. It is also sufficient to consider
0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
for any fixed
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(q)>0
.

With
c_{0}
to be specified, define

(3.26)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}m}=\inf\left\{u>0:\sigma_{u}\log u>\frac{\epsilon\mu s}{16c_{0}}\right\}\wedge\frac{r}{2},\quad{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}t}=\epsilon\Phi(m)^{1/2},

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}S_{1}}=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:r+s-m\leq w_{1}\leq r+s-m+2,D_{r+s}(w)\leq t\},

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}S_{2}}=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:r+s-m\leq w_{1}\leq r+s-m+2,|w^{*}|\leq\epsilon m\}.

Note that provided
C_{55}
(and hence
s
and
m
) is large, which we henceforth tacitly assume, and provided
\epsilon_{0}
is small, we have
\Phi^{-1}(t)<m/2
and
m\geq 2s
.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}w}\in S_{1}
. We have
|w_{1}-(r+s)|\geq m-2>\Phi^{-1}(t)
(meaning
w
lies to the left of the cylinder around
(r+s)e_{1}
in the tube-and-cylinders region
\{u:D_{r+s}(u)\leq t\}
) and so by (
3.18
),

(3.27)

\frac{|w^{*}|}{m}\leq\frac{t^{1/2}\Xi(m)}{m}\leq\frac{t}{\Phi(m)^{1/2}}=\epsilon.

Thus
S_{1}\subset S_{2}
.
We let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{w}}=(r+s-m,w^{*})
, so
|w-\hat{w}|\leq 2
for
w\in S_{2}
. See Figure
1
. Then for such
w
, recalling
m\geq 2s
, we have

|\hat{w}-(r+s)e_{1}|-|\hat{w}-re_{1}|=(m^{2}+|w^{*}|^{2})^{1/2}-((m-s)^{2}+|w^{*}|^{2})^{1/2}\geq\frac{sm}{(m^{2}+|w^{*}|^{2})^{1/2}}\geq(1-\epsilon^{2})s,

so assuming
C_{55}
is large and
\epsilon_{0}
is small,

(3.28)

|w-(r+s)e_{1}|-|w-re_{1}|\geq(1-\epsilon^{2})s-2\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{4}\right)s.

Also for
w\in S_{2}
,
m/2\leq m-s\leq m-2\leq|w-re_{1}|\leq 2m
and hence by (
1.10
),
\sigma(|w-re_{1}|)\leq 3C_{23}\sigma_{m-2}
. It follows that provided we choose
c_{0}
large enough in (
3.26
),

C_{46}\sigma(|w-re_{1}|)\log|w-re_{1}|\leq 4C_{23}C_{46}\sigma_{m-2}\log(m-2)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{12}\mu s.

With (
3.28
) and Proposition
3.3
, this yields that provided
C_{55}
is large,

\displaystyle h(|w-(r+s)e_{1}|)-h(|w-re_{1}|)

\displaystyle\geq\mu|w-(r+s)e_{1}|-\mu|w-re_{1}|-C_{46}\sigma(|w-re_{1}|)\log|w-re_{1}|

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{3}\right)\mu s

(3.29)

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)h(s).

Figure 1.
Diagram for the proof of Lemma
3.6
.
S_{1}
is shaded.

We need a lower bound for
t
. Since
q>{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{q}}=(1+\chi)/(1-\chi)
, we can choose
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}b}>0
small enough so
(q-1)(1-b)^{2}>(1+b)(\hat{q}-1)
.
Provided
C_{55}
is large enough (in the lemma statement, and depending on
b,q
), since
s\geq C_{55}\epsilon^{-q}
we have

(3.30)

m\geq(\epsilon s)^{(1-b)/\chi},\quad\Phi(m)\geq m^{(1-b)(1-\chi)},\quad t=\epsilon\Phi(m)^{1/2}\geq c_{1}\epsilon^{1-(1-b)^{2}(q-1)(1-\chi)/2\chi}\geq c_{1}\epsilon^{-b}.

Observe that for every vertex
w\in\Gamma_{0,(r+s)e_{1}}\cap\mathbb{V}
we have

(3.31)

T(0,(r+s)e_{1})-T(0,re_{1})=T(0,w)+T(w,(r+s)e_{1})-T(0,re_{1})\geq T(w,(r+s)e_{1})-T(w,re_{1}).

Let

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}T^{*}}=\min\{T(w,(r+s)e_{1})-T(w,re_{1}):w\in S_{2}\cap V\}

and let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}W}
be the first vertex in
\Gamma_{0,(r+s)e_{1}}\cap\mathbb{V}
satisfying
r+s-m\leq W_{1}\leq r+s-m+2
.
If
W\in S_{2}
then by (
3.31
) and subadditivity we have

T(0,(r+s)e_{1})-T(0,re_{1})\geq T^{*},\qquad|T(0,(r+s)e_{1})-T(0,re_{1})|\leq T(re_{1},(r+s)e_{1}),

|T^{*}|\leq T(re_{1},(r+s)e_{1}).

Hence

\displaystyle h(r+s)-h(r)

\displaystyle=E[T(0,(r+s)e_{1})-T(0,re_{1})]

\displaystyle\geq E(T^{*}1_{\{W\in S_{2}\}})-E(T(re_{1},(r+s)e_{1})1_{\{W\notin S_{2}\}})

(3.32)

\displaystyle\geq E(T^{*})-2E(T(re_{1},(r+s)e_{1})1_{\{W\notin S_{2}\}}).

It follows from (
1.3
) that for some
c_{2}

E(T(re_{1},(r+s)e_{1})^{2})^{1/2}\leq c_{2}s.

Assuming
\epsilon_{0}
is small, by Proposition
3.4
and (
3.30
) we have

(3.33)

P(W\notin S_{2})\leq P(W\notin S_{1})\leq C_{47}e^{-C_{48}t\log t}\leq C_{47}e^{-c_{3}\epsilon^{-b}}\leq\left(\frac{\epsilon\mu}{8c_{2}}\right)^{2}.

Combining these yields

(3.34)

E(T(re_{1},(r+s)e_{1})1_{\{W\notin S_{2}\}})\leq E(T(re_{1},(r+s)e_{1})^{2})^{1/2}P(W\notin S_{2})^{1/2}\leq\frac{\epsilon\mu s}{8}.

To use (
3
) we also need a lower bound for
E(T^{*})
. For
y>0
, using (
3
),

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\left(T^{*}\leq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)h(s)-2y\sigma_{m}\log m\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq P\Bigg(\text{for some }w\in S_{2}\cap\mathbb{V},\ T(w,(r+s)e_{1})-T(w,re_{1})\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad h(|w-(r+s)e_{1}|)-h(|w-re_{1}|)-2y\sigma_{m}\log m\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq P\Bigg(\text{for some }w\in S_{2}\cap\mathbb{V},\ T(w,(r+s)e_{1})-h(|w-(r+s)e_{1}|)\leq-y\sigma_{m}\log m\Bigg)

(3.35)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+P\Bigg(\text{for some }w\in S_{2}\cap\mathbb{V},\ T(w,re_{1})-h(|w-re_{1}|)\geq y\sigma_{m}\log m\Bigg).

We consider the first probability on the right in (
3
); the second probability is similar. For
w\in S_{2}
we have using (
3.27
) that

m-2\leq|w-(r+s)e_{1}|\leq\left(1+\epsilon^{2}\right)m,

and then from (
1.10
),

\frac{\sigma_{m}}{\sigma(|w-(r+s)e_{1}|)}\geq\frac{C_{22}}{2}.

From these and Lemma
3.2
(see
C_{45}
there) we obtain that if
c_{0}
is large enough, then for
y\geq c_{0}
,

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\Bigg(\text{for some }w\in S_{2}\cap\mathbb{V},\ T(w,(r+s)e_{1})-h(|w-(r+s)e_{1}|)\leq-y\sigma_{m}\log m\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\hat{w}\in S_{2}\cap d^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}P\Bigg(\text{for some }w\in B_{1}(\hat{w})\cap\mathbb{V},

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464ptT(w,(r+s)e_{1})-h(|\hat{w}-(r+s)e_{1}|)\leq h(|w-\hat{w}|)-y\sigma_{m}\log m\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\hat{w}\in S_{2}\cap d^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}}P\Bigg(\text{for some }w\in B_{1}(\hat{w})\cap\mathbb{V},\ T(w,(r+s)e_{1})-h(|\hat{w}-(r+s)e_{1}|)\leq-\frac{y}{2}\sigma_{m}\log m\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}m^{d-1}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{45}C_{22}y}{4}\log m\right)

(3.36)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{-c_{5}y\log m}.

The same bound holds for the second probability on the right in (
3
).
With (
3
) and the definition of
m
in (
3.26
) this shows that

ET^{*}\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)h(s)-4c_{0}\sigma_{m}\log m\geq\left(1-\frac{3\epsilon}{4}\right)h(s).

Combining this with (
3
) and (
3.34
) yields (
3.24
).

We now prove (ii). From (i), there exists
c_{6}
such that
h(r+s)\geq h(r)+(1-\epsilon)h(s)
whenever
r,s\geq c_{6}
; there then exists
c_{7}
such that
h(s)\leq c_{7}
whenever
0\leq s\leq c_{6}
. We therefore have

h(r+s)\geq\begin{cases}h(r)+(1-\epsilon)h(s)&\text{if }r,s\geq c_{6}\\
h(r)-h(s)\geq h(r)+(1-\epsilon)h(s)-2c_{7}&\text{if }s<c_{6}\\
0\geq h(r)+(1-\epsilon)h(s)-2c_{7}&\text{if }r<c_{6}\end{cases}

which proves (
3.25
).
∎

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
—downward deviations

We use a multiscale argument which is related to chaining. But first we dispense with simpler cases that only require Lemma
3.2
and Proposition
3.4
. The first such case is pairs
x,y
which are close together. For technical convenience later, we prove the following also for geodesics with endpoints in the set
G_{r}^{+}
, satisfying
G_{r}^{+}\supset G_{r}(K)
for
K
fixed and
r
large, given by

(4.1)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}G_{r}^{+}}=[-C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)},r+C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}]\times C_{60}\Delta_{r}(\log r)\mathfrak{B}_{d-1},

with
C_{60}
to be specified.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. There exist constants
C_{i}

for all
r\geq 2,K\geq 1,
and
t>C_{61}K^{2}
,

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\left(T(x,y)\leq ET(x,y)-t\sigma_{r}\text{ for some $x,y\in G_{r}(K)\cup G_{r}^{+}$ with $|y-x|\leq\frac{C_{62}r}{(\log r)^{1/\chi_{1}}}$}\right)

(4.2)

\displaystyle\leq C_{63}e^{-C_{64}t\log r}.

Proof.

We may assume
t
is large. We first discretize: by Lemma
3.1
(ii), for
x\in B_{1}(\hat{x}),y\in B_{1}(\hat{y})
, we have
|ET(x,y)-ET(\hat{x},\hat{y})|\leq 2C_{42}\leq t\sigma_{r}/2
, so for
c_{0}>0
,

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\left(T(x,y)\leq ET(x,y)-t\sigma_{r}\text{ for some $x,y\in G_{r}^{+}$ with $|y-x|\leq\frac{c_{0}r}{(\log r)^{1/\chi_{1}}}$}\right)

(4.3)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{x},\hat{y}\in d^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap(G_{r}(K)\cup G_{r}^{+})\\
|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|\leq 2c_{0}r/(\log r)^{1/\chi_{1}}\end{subarray}}P\left(T(x,y)\leq ET(\hat{x},\hat{y})-\frac{t\sigma_{r}}{2}\text{ for some $x\in B_{1}(\hat{x}),y\in B_{1}(\hat{y})$}\right).

From (
1.10
),

|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|\leq\frac{2c_{0}r}{(\log r)^{1/\chi_{1}}}\implies\frac{\sigma(r)}{\sigma(|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|)}\geq\frac{c_{1}}{(2c_{0})^{1/\chi_{1}}}\log r,

so if we take
c_{0}
small enough then by Lemma
3.2
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{x},\hat{y}\in d^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap(G_{r}(K)\cup G_{r}^{+})\\
|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|\leq c_{0}r/2(\log r)^{1/\chi_{1}}\end{subarray}}P\left(T(x,y)\leq ET(\hat{x},\hat{y})-\frac{t\sigma_{r}}{2}\text{ for some $x\in B_{1}(\hat{x}),y\in B_{1}(\hat{y})$}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c_{2}r(K\Delta_{r}\log r)^{d-1}\max\left\{e^{-C_{45}t\sigma_{r}/2\sigma(|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|)}:|\hat{x}-\hat{y}|\leq c_{0}r/2(\log r)^{1/\chi_{1}}\right\}

(4.4)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq e^{-C_{45}t\log r},

which with (
4
) proves (
4.1
).
∎

Lemma
4.1
means we need only consider
x,y\in G_{r}(K)
satisfying

(4.5)

|y-x|>\frac{C_{62}r}{(\log r)^{1/\chi_{1}}}.

Writing
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\alpha_{uv}}
for the angle between nonzero vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, this means that for large
r
,

(4.6)

\alpha_{y-x,e_{1}}\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\beta_{r}}:=c_{0}\frac{\Delta_{r}(\log r)^{1/\chi_{1}}}{r}\leq r^{-(1-\chi)/4}.

A second simple case is small
r
. For fixed
r_{0}
and
1\leq r\leq r_{0}
, from Lemma
3.1
for all
x,y\in G_{r}
we have
ET(x,y)\leq c_{1}r
, so for
t\geq c_{2}r_{0}
we have
ET(x,y)-t\sigma_{r}\leq c_{1}r-t\sigma_{r}<0
, and hence the probability in (
1.13
) is 0. Therefore there exist
C_{29},C_{30}
such that (
1.13
) is valid for all
1\leq r\leq r_{0}
and
t>0
.

A third simple case is
t\geq c_{3}\log(Kr)
, with
c_{3}
large enough. As in (
4
) and (
4
), for
C_{45}
from Lemma
3.2
we then have

\displaystyle P\left(T(x,y)\leq ET(x,y)-t\sigma_{r}\text{ for some $x,y\in G_{r}(K)$}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}r(K\Delta_{r})^{d-1}\max_{u,v\in G_{r}(K)}e^{-C_{45}t\sigma_{r}/2\sigma(|u-v|)}

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}r(K\Delta_{r})^{d-1}e^{-c_{5}t}

(4.7)

\displaystyle\leq c_{6}e^{-c_{5}t/2}.

It follows that, for
C_{26}
from Theorem
1.3
, we need only consider
C_{26}K^{2}\leq t<c_{3}\log(Kr)
, and therefore also
K\leq c_{7}(\log r)^{1/2}
, which means
G_{r}(2K)\subset G_{r}^{+}
.

A fourth simple case is pairs
x,y
for which
\Gamma_{xy}
goes well outside
G_{r}(K)
, when
r
is large and
t<c_{3}\log(Kr)
. Assuming
r
is large,
C=2c_{3}
, from the third simple case, is the choice of interest in the following.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}=(\mathbb{V},\mathbb{E})
and
\{\eta_{e},e\in\mathbb{E}\}
satisfy A1, A2, and A3. Then given
C>0
there exist constants
C_{i}=C_{i}(C)
such that for
c_{7}
as above, for all
r\geq C_{65},K\leq c_{7}(\log r)^{1/2},C_{66}K^{2}\leq t\leq C\log(Kr)
we have

P\Big(\Gamma_{xy}\not\subset G_{r}^{+}\text{ for some $x,y\in G_{r}(K)$ satisfying \eqref{xyfar}}\Big)\leq C_{68}e^{-C_{69}t}.

Proof.

Note the assumptions guarantee
G_{r}(K)\subset G_{r}^{+}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{x},\hat{y}}\in d^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap G_{r}(K)
with
\hat{x}_{1}<\hat{y}_{1}
, and let
x,y\in G_{r}
satisfy (
4.5
), with
|x-\hat{x}|<1,|y-\hat{y}|<1
.
Recall the transformation
\Theta_{\hat{x}\hat{y}}
which takes
\Pi_{\hat{x}\hat{y}}
to
\Pi_{0,|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|e_{1}}
.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}w}\in\Gamma_{\hat{x}\hat{y}}\backslash G_{r}^{+}
and let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{w}}=\Theta_{\hat{x}\hat{y}}w
; we may take
w
with
d(w,G_{r}^{+})\leq 2
. We claim that, for
D_{r}
from (
3.20
), we have
D_{r}(\tilde{w})\geq C_{60}\log r
, with
C_{60}
from the definition of
G_{r}^{+}
. We consider several cases.

Case 1.

-C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}\leq w_{1}\leq r+C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}
, that is,
w
is to the side of
G_{r}^{+}
. See Figure
2
. From symmetry we may assume
-C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}\leq w_{1}\leq r/2
. Note that in the definition (
3.17
) of
D(u)
, the case
u_{1}\geq 0
is the relevant one here if and only if
\tilde{w}_{1}\in[0,|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|]
.
We have

(4.8)

d(\tilde{w},\Pi_{0,|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|e_{1}})=d(w,\Pi_{\hat{x}\hat{y}})\geq d(w,G_{r}(K))\geq\frac{C_{60}}{2}\Delta_{r}\log r,\qquad|w^{*}|\geq C_{60}\Delta_{r}\log r,

and
\tilde{w}_{1}\in[0,|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|]\implies d(\tilde{w},\Pi_{0,|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|e_{1}})=|\tilde{w}^{*}|
, so

\tilde{w}_{1}\in\left[0,\frac{|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|}{2}\right]\implies\frac{|\tilde{w}^{*}|^{2}}{\Xi(\tilde{w}_{1})^{2}}\geq\frac{d(\tilde{w},\Pi_{0,|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|e_{1}})^{2}}{\Xi\left(\frac{1}{2}|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|\right)^{2}}\geq\frac{C_{60}^{2}\Delta_{r}^{2}(\log r)^{2}}{4\Xi(r)^{2}}\geq\frac{C_{60}^{2}}{5}\log r.

Also as in (
4.8
),

\max(|\tilde{w}_{1}|,|\tilde{w}^{*}|)\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|\tilde{w}|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|w-\hat{x}|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}d(w,\Pi_{\hat{x}\hat{y}})\geq\frac{C_{60}}{3}\Delta_{r}\log r

so

\Phi\left(\max\big(|\tilde{w}_{1}|,|\tilde{w}^{*}|\big)\right)\geq C_{60}\log r.

Therefore under Case 1,

(4.9)

-\infty<\tilde{w}_{1}\leq\frac{|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|}{2}\implies D_{|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|}(\tilde{w})\geq C_{60}\log r,

and symmetrically the same holds for
\tilde{w}_{1}>|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|/2
.

Case 2.

w_{1}<-C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}
with
\tilde{w}_{1}>0
, or
w_{1}>r+C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}
with
\tilde{w}_{1}<|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|
, that is,
w
is past the end of
G_{r}^{+}
but
\tilde{w}
is to the side of
\Pi_{0,|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|e_{1}}
. See Figure
2
. The two ends are symmetric so we need only consider
w_{1}<-C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}
with
\tilde{w}_{1}>0
. Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}H_{(\hat{x})}}
be the hyperplane through
\hat{x}
perpendicular to
\hat{y}-\hat{x}
, and let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overline{w}}
be the orthogonal projection of
w
into
H_{(\hat{x})}
, so
|\overline{w}-\hat{x}|=|\tilde{w}^{*}|
and
|w-\overline{w}|=|\tilde{w}_{1}|
. Since
K\leq c_{7}(\log r)^{1/2}
, by (
4.6
) the angle
\alpha_{\hat{y}-\hat{x},e_{1}}
is small enough that we have
\overline{w}_{1}<w_{1}<0
and hence, for
\beta_{r}
from (
4.6
),

(4.10)

C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}\leq\hat{x}_{1}-\overline{w}_{1}\leq|\overline{w}-\hat{x}|\sin\alpha_{\hat{y}-\hat{x},e_{1}}\leq|\tilde{w}^{*}|\beta_{r};

therefore

\Phi\left(\max\big(|\tilde{w}_{1}|,|\tilde{w}^{*}|\big)\right)\geq\Phi\left(\frac{C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}}{\beta_{r}}\right)\geq C_{60}\log r.

Further, similarly to (
4.10
), since the small angle
\alpha_{\hat{y}-\hat{x},e_{1}}
ensures
\frac{1}{2}\tilde{w}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}|w-\overline{w}|\leq(w-\overline{w})_{1}
, we have

(4.11)

\frac{1}{2}\tilde{w}_{1}\leq(w-\overline{w})_{1}\leq(\hat{x}-\overline{w})_{1}=|\overline{w}-\hat{x}|\sin\alpha_{\hat{y}-\hat{x},e_{1}}\leq|\tilde{w}^{*}|\beta_{r}

and therefore using (
1.10
),

\frac{|\tilde{w}^{*}|^{2}}{\Xi(\tilde{w}_{1})^{2}}\geq\frac{|\tilde{w}^{*}|^{2}}{\Xi(2\beta_{r}|\tilde{w}^{*}|)^{2}}\geq\frac{C_{22}|\tilde{w}^{*}|}{\beta_{r}^{\chi_{1}}\sigma(|\tilde{w}^{*}|)\log(2+|\tilde{w}^{*}|)}\geq\frac{1}{\beta_{r}^{\chi_{1}}}\geq C_{60}\log r.

This proves the right side of (
4.9
) under Case 2.

Figure 2.
Diagram for Case 1 (right) and Case 2 (left) in the proof of Lemma
4.2
. On the left, if we translate and rotate the picture so that
\hat{x}
becomes 0 and
\hat{y}-\hat{x}
becomes horizontal, then
w
becomes
\tilde{w}
. Case 3 is like Case 2 except that
\tilde{w}
is to the left of 0.

Case 3.

w_{1}<-C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}
with
\tilde{w}_{1}\leq 0
, or
w_{1}>r+C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}
with
\tilde{w}_{1}\geq|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|
, that is,
w
is past the end of
G_{r}^{+}
and
\tilde{w}
is past the end of
\Pi_{0,|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|e_{1}}
. The two ends are again symmetric so we need only consider
w_{1}<-C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}
with
\tilde{w}_{1}\leq 0
. Then
|\tilde{w}|=|w-\hat{x}|\geq\hat{x}_{1}-w_{1}\geq C_{60}(\log r)^{2/(1-\chi)}
so

D_{r}(\tilde{w})=\Phi\left(\max\big(|\tilde{w}_{1}|,|\tilde{w}^{*}|\big)\right)\geq\Phi\left(\frac{|\tilde{w}|}{2}\right)\geq C_{60}\log r.

Thus is all cases we have
D_{r}(\tilde{w})\geq C_{60}\log r
,
so by Proposition
3.4
,

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\Big(\Gamma_{xy}\not\subset G_{r}^{+}\text{ for some $x,y\in G_{r}(K)$ satisfying \eqref{xyfar}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\hat{x},\hat{y}\in d^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap G_{r}(K)}P\Big(\Gamma_{xy}\not\subset G_{r}^{+}\text{ for some $x,y\in G_{r}(K)$ satisfying \eqref{xyfar} with }|x-\hat{x}|<1,|y-\hat{y}|<1\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\hat{x},\hat{y}\in d^{-1/2}\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap G_{r}(K)}P\left(\sup_{x\in B_{1}(\hat{x})\cap\mathbb{V},y\in B_{1}(\hat{y})\cap\mathbb{V}}\ \sup_{w\in\Gamma_{xy}}D_{|\hat{y}-\hat{x}|}(\Theta_{\hat{x}\hat{y}}w)\geq C_{60}\log r\right)

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}r(K\Delta_{r})^{d-1}e^{-C_{47}(\log\log r)\log r}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}e^{-c_{2}t}.

∎

In summary, we may restrict to the following situation (still with
x,y\in G_{r}(K)
):

(4.12)

r\geq r_{0},\quad K\leq c_{7}(\log r)^{1/2},\quad c_{8}\leq t\leq c_{3}\log r,\quad|y-x|>\frac{C_{62}r}{(\log r)^{1/\chi_{1}}},\quad\Gamma_{xy}\subset G_{r}^{+},

with
C_{62}
from Lemma
4.1
.

4.1.
Step 1. Setting up the coarse-graining.

For purposes of coarse–graining and multiscale analysis of paths, we build grids inside
G_{r}^{+}
on various scales, using small parameters
\lambda,\delta,\beta
satsfying

(4.13)

1\gg\lambda\gg\delta^{\chi_{1}}\gg\delta^{(1+\chi_{1})/2}\gg\beta

in the sense that the ratio of each term to the one following must be taken sufficiently large, in a manner to be specified. We choose these so
1/\delta
and
1/\beta
are integers. There is also a fourth parameter
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rho}>1
, and we further require

(4.14)

\frac{\beta}{\lambda\rho\delta^{(1+\chi_{2})/2}}\ll 1,\quad\frac{\rho\beta}{\lambda\delta^{(1-\chi_{1})/2}}\ll 1,\quad\frac{\beta^{2}}{\lambda^{2}\delta}\ll 1,\quad\frac{\beta^{2\chi_{1}/(1+\chi_{1})}}{\lambda\delta^{3}}\ll 1,\quad\rho^{2}\lambda\gg 1;

all of (
4.14
) can be satisfied by taking
\beta
small enough after choosing
\rho,\lambda,\delta
.
For
j\geq 1
, a
jth–scale hyperplane
is one of form
H_{k\delta^{j}r},k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the
jth–scale grid
in
G_{r}^{+}
is

\mathbb{L}_{j}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbb{L}_{j}(r)}=\left\{u\in G_{r}^{+}:u_{1}\in\delta^{j}r\mathbb{Z},u^{*}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}K_{0}}\beta^{j}\Delta_{r}\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}\right\},

where
K_{0}\in[1,2]
is to be specified.
Note that larger
j
values correspond to smaller scales, and since
1/\delta
is an integer, a
j
th–scale hyperplane for some
j
is also a
k
th–scale hyperplane for
k>j
. A hyperplane is
maximally jth–scale
if it is
j
th–scale but not
(j-1)
th–scale.

The
j
th–scale grid divides a
j
th–scale hyperplane into cubes which we call
jth–scale blocks
. For concreteness we take these blocks to be products of left–open–right–closed intervals. Each point
u
of the hyperplane then lies in a unique such block.
For a point
u
in a
j
th–scale hyperplane, the
jth–scale coarse–grain approximation of

u
is the point
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}V_{j}(u)}
which is the unique corner point in the block containing
u
. We abbreviate coarse–grain as CG. The definition ensures that two points with the same
j
th–scale CG approximation also have the same
k
th–scale CG approximation for all larger scales
k<j
.

A
transverse step
in the
j
th–scale grid is a step from some
u\in\mathbb{L}_{j}
to some
v\in\mathbb{L}_{j}
satisfying
v_{1}=u_{1},|v^{*}-u^{*}|=K_{0}\beta^{j}\Delta_{r}
; a
longitudinal step
is from
u
to
v
satisfying
v_{1}=u_{1}+\delta^{j}r,v^{*}=u^{*}
. Define

\ell_{1}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell_{1}(j)}=\frac{\Delta(\delta^{j}r)}{\beta^{j}\Delta(r)},\qquad\ell_{2}={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\ell_{2}(j)}=\rho^{j}\ell_{1}(j),

so from (
1.10
),

(4.15)

\ell_{1}\geq\frac{\delta^{(1+\chi_{2})j/2}}{C_{23}\beta^{j}},\qquad\frac{\delta^{(1+\chi_{2})j/2}\rho^{j}}{C_{23}\beta^{j}}\leq\ell_{2}\leq\frac{\delta^{(1+\chi_{1})j/2}\rho^{j}}{C_{22}\beta^{j}}.

Here
\ell_{1}(j)
is chosen so that the typical transverse fluctuation
\Delta(\delta^{j}r)
for a geodesic making one longitudinal step is
\ell_{1}(j)
transverse steps.

On short enough length scales, coarse–graining is unnecessary because we can use Proposition
3.5
and Lemma
4.1
. More precisely, we will need only consider
j\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}j_{1}}=j_{1}(r)
where
j_{1}
is the least
j
for which

(4.16)

\left(\frac{\lambda}{\delta^{\chi_{1}}}\right)^{j_{1}}\geq(\log r)^{2},\quad\text{so}\quad j_{1}(r)\asymp\log\log r.

Provided
r
is large, this means the spacings
\delta^{j_{1}}r
and
K_{0}\beta^{j_{1}}\Delta_{r}
of the
j_{1}
th–scale grid are large, and therefore
we can choose
